Derived binomial rings I: integral Betti cohomology of log schemes
Dmitry Kubrak, Georgii Shuklin, Alexander Zakharov

Abstract
We introduce and study a derived version 𝐋 ​ Bin \mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin} of the binomial monad on the unbounded derived category 𝒟 ⁡ ( Z ) \mathscr{D}(\mathbb Z) of Z \mathbb Z -modules. This monad acts naturally on singular cohomology of any topological space, and does so more efficiently than the more classical monad 𝐋 ​ Sym Z \mathbf{L}\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z} . We compute all free derived binomial rings on abelian groups concentrated in a single degree, in particular identifying C ∗ sing ​ ( K ⁡ ( Z , n ) , Z ) C_{*}^{\mathrm{sing}}(K(\mathbb Z,n),\mathbb Z) with 𝐋 ​ Bin ​ ( Z ⁡ [ − n ] ) \mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(\mathbb Z[-n]) via a different argument than in [ Toë20 ] and [ Hor22 ] . Using this we show that the singular cohomology functor C ∗ sing ​ ( − , Z ) C_{*}^{\mathrm{sing}}(-,\mathbb Z) induces a fully faithful embedding of the category of connected nilpotent spaces of finite type to the category of derived binomial rings. We then also define a version 𝐋 ​ ℬ ​ i ​ n X \mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}in_{X} of the derived binomial monad on the ∞ \infty -category of 𝒟 ⁡ ( Z ) \mathscr{D}(\mathbb Z) -valued sheaves on a sufficiently nice topological space X X . As an application we give a closed formula for the singular cohomology of an fs log complex analytic space ( X , ℳ ) (X,\mathcal{M}) : namely we identify the pushforward R ​ π ∗ ​ Z ¯ R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z} for the corresponding Kato-Nakayama space π : X log → X \pi\colon X^{\log}\rightarrow X with the free coaugmented derived binomial ring on the 2-term exponential complex 𝒪 X → ℳ gr \mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\gr} . This gives an extension of Steenbrink’s formula and its generalization in [ Shu22 ] to Z \mathbb Z -coefficients.

Contents

1
Introduction

1.1
Spoilers

This project grew as an attempt to explain in a reasonable way the isomorphism of graded algebras (
[
KP22
, Appendix A]
)

H^{*}_{{\mathrm{sing}}}(K(\mathbb Z,3),\mathbb Z)\simeq\oplus_{*_{1}+*_{2}=*}H^{*_{1}}(B\mathbb G_{a,\mathbb Z},\mathcal{O})_{*_{2}},

somewhat strangely relating the coherent cohomology of the classifying stack
B\mathbb G_{a}
over
\mathbb Z
(purely algebraic data) with singular cohomology of the Eilenberg-MacLane space
K(\mathbb Z,3)
(topological data). Moreover it is doing so via some weird shifts of cohomological degrees and degrees of stackiness (if one views
K(\mathbb Z,3)
as
B^{3}\mathbb Z
in the homotopy-theoretic setting). Here the extra
*_{2}
-grading on
H^{*_{1}}(B\mathbb G_{a,\mathbb Z},\mathcal{O})
is induced by the action of
\mathbb G_{m}
on
\mathbb G_{a}
via quadratic rescaling
1
1

1

Meaning that the corresponding map
\mathbb G_{m}\times\mathbb G_{a}\rightarrow\mathbb G_{a}
is given by
(t,x)\mapsto t^{2}x\in\mathbb G_{a}
, see
[
KP22
, Appendix A]
for more details.
.

In the end, this isomorphism turns out to be naturally explained via the trivialization of a certain deformation from functor
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}
to functor
\mathbf{L}\Gamma
, when evaluated on
\mathbb Z[-3]\in{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb Z)
. However, a detailed study of this deformation (and its generalizations) is exactly what we decided to defer to the sequel of this paper. So, without further ado, let us switch to describing the actual contents of the current text.

1.2
Binomial rings and homotopy theory: a short historical overview

Let us first recall the notion of a classical binomial ring, following the work of
[
Hal57
]
and a later revisit by Elliot
[
Ell06
]
.

Definition 1.1
.

A commutative ring
B
is called a
binomial ring
if it is torsion free as a
\mathbb Z
-module and for each
b\in B
and
n\in\mathbb N
the binomial coefficient

{\tbinom{b}{n}}\coloneqq\tfrac{b(b-1)\cdots(b-n+1)}{n!}\in B\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q

lies in
B\subset B\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q
.

While any
\mathbb Q
-algebra obviously gives a binomial ring, it turns out that the condition in
1.1
imposes some rather strong restrictions on the arithmetic of
B
, for example the absolute Frobenius on
B/p
is automatically trivial
[
Ell06
, Theorem 4.1(4)]
. In fact one can fully classify binomial rings as a certain subclass of torsion-free
\lambda
-rings: namely those with all Adams operations being trivial (see
[
Wil82
]
). The main examples are given by the ring of integers
\mathbb Z
and, more generally, the ring of
\mathbb Z
-valued functions
\Map_{\mathrm{Set}}(S,\mathbb Z)
on a set
S
.

There has been some history of using binomial rings in homotopy theory; in one way or the other all records seem to be related to attempts of developing some relatively explicit algebraic models for
H\mathbb Z
-localization of spaces, see e.g.
[
Wil76
]
,
[
Eke02
]
, as well as recent works,
[
Toë20
]
and
[
Hor22
]
. The idea is usually to associate to
X\in{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{pc}}

“polynomial singular cochains”
in a certain sense, and then endow them with a suitable homotopy algebraic structure to obtain a fully faithful embedding from some subcategory of spaces. For
H\mathbb Q
-localization such desirable model was constructed by Sullivan
[
Sul77
]
, by associating to a simplicial set the corresponding DG-algebra
\Omega^{*}_{\mathrm{PL}}(X)
of polynomial
\mathbb Q
-differential forms; unfortunately, blindly replacing
\mathbb Q
with
\mathbb Z
in his construction fails immediately due to non-acyclicity of de Rham complex of
\mathbb A^{1}
over
\mathbb Z
.

While for general space
X\in{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{pc}}
, say represented by a simplicial set
X_{\bullet}
, there does not seem to exist a nice explicit integral algebraic model generalizing
\Omega^{*}_{\mathrm{PL}}(X)
, there is a natural candidate in the case of an Eilenberg-MacLane space
K(A,n)
where
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
is a lattice
2
2

2

As usual by lattice we mean a finitely generated free abelian group.
.

Recall that
K(A,n)\in{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{pc}}
can be computed as the (geometric realization of the) underlying simplicial set of the simplicial abelian group
\mathrm{DK}(A[n])_{\bullet}
that corresponds to the complex
A[n]
via Dold-Kan correspondence. More explicitly,

\mathrm{DK}(A[n])_{m}\simeq\bigoplus_{\alpha:[m]\twoheadrightarrow[n]}A\simeq A^{\oplus\binom{m}{n}}\in\mathrm{Lat}

with naturally defined face and degeneration maps. Its singular cohomology
C^{*}_{\mathrm{sing}}(K(A,n),\mathbb Z)
then is explicitly described as the totalization of the cosimplicial algebra

\Map_{\mathrm{Set}}(\mathrm{DK}(A[n])_{\bullet},\mathbb Z)=\Map_{\mathrm{Set}}(A^{\oplus\binom{\bullet}{n}},\mathbb Z)

of all set-theoretic maps from
\mathrm{DK}(A[n])_{\bullet}
to
\mathbb Z
. Now, using the abelian group structure on
A
, one can consider a smaller cosimplicial algebra:

\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(\mathrm{DK}(A[n])_{\bullet},\mathbb Z)\subset\Map_{\mathrm{Set}}(\mathrm{DK}(A[n])_{\bullet},\mathbb Z)

given by those maps
\mathrm{DK}(A[n])_{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathbb Z
that are
polynomial
in the sense of difference operators (see
2.1
), or, equivalently are represented by polynomials with rational coefficients. The key observation, which seems to be due to Ekedahl (
[
Eke02
]
), is that if
n\geq 1
one can also use
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(\mathrm{DK}(A[n])_{\bullet},\mathbb Z)
to compute singular cohomology of
K(A,n)
, namely,

C_{\mathrm{sing}}^{*}(K(A,n),\mathbb Z)\simeq\Tot_{[\bullet]\in\Delta}\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(\mathrm{DK}(A[n])_{\bullet},\mathbb Z).

(1.1)

One can speculate that the cosimplicial algebra on the right is exactly the
“polynomial cochains”
on
K(A,n)
in a certain sense.

Now let us indicate how binomial algebras, or rather their derived version, naturally come in handy here. One first observes that for any lattice
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
, algebras
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)
and
\Map_{{\mathrm{Set}}}(A,\mathbb Z)
are binomial; moreover,
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)
is the free binomial algebra
3
3

3

Meaning that for any binomial algebra
B
the set of maps
\Map_{\BinAlg}(\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z),B)
is canonically identified with
\Map_{\mathrm{Ab}}(A^{\vee},B)
in the category of abelian groups.
on the
\mathbb Z
-linear dual
A^{\vee}
. This allows to identify the right hand side in
Equation

1.1
with the free derived binomial algebra on
\Tot(\mathrm{DK}(A[n])_{\bullet}^{\vee})\simeq A^{\vee}[-n]
, giving canonical equivalence

C_{\mathrm{sing}}^{*}(K(A,n),\mathbb Z)\simeq\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(A^{\vee}[-n])

as (appropriately defined) binomial algebras. This allows to effectively describe maps from
C_{\mathrm{sing}}^{*}(K(A,n),\mathbb Z)
considered as a derived binomial ring.
In particular, for any space
X
the mapping space between derived binomial rings
C_{\mathrm{sing}}^{*}(K(A,n),\mathbb Z)
and
C_{\mathrm{sing}}^{*}(X,\mathbb Z)
is naturally identified with the mapping space between
X
and
K(A,n)
in the category of spaces. This ultimately allows to show (see e.g.
[
Hor22
]
, or
Section

3.2
) that the functor
X\mapsto C_{\mathrm{sing}}^{*}(X,\mathbb Z)
from
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{pc}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}
to
\DBinAlg
is fully faithful when restricted to the subcategory spanned by nilpotent spaces
of finite type.

1.3
Derived binomial rings: current work

Hopefully having sufficiently motivated the reader with the notion of derived binomial algebras, let us now describe more concretely what we do in this paper. First of all, taking a slightly more general approach than
[
Hor22
]
, in
Section

2
we define the derived binomial monad
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}
on the full derived category
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb Z)
, rather than the coconnective part
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb Z)^{\geq 0}
. In doing so we use the approach of
[
Rak20
]
via
filtered monads
: namely we first define a filtered monad
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq*}
on the category
\mathrm{Lat}
of lattices, then extend it first to
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb Z)^{\leq 0}
via the animation procedure. Then, using the polynomiality of individual functors
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}
, we extend it to the full derived category
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb Z)
following
[
BM19
]
,
[
Rak20
]
(which in turn go back to
[
Ill72
]
). We define filtered monad
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq*}
in terms of polynomial maps between abelian groups, with a hope that our exposition will shed more light on the relation between our work and the work of Ekedahl
[
Eke02
]
. This also allows to describe in more functorial way the individual functors
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}\colon\mathrm{Lat}\rightarrow\mathrm{Lat}
.

After the monad
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}
is constructed we define the
\infty
-category
\DBinAlg
of derived binomial rings as the corresponding category of modules. When restricted to the subcategory
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
of torsion-free classical abelian groups,
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}
recovers the classical binomial monad
\mathrm{Bin}
that was studied in
[
Ell06
]
. This way the category of classical binomial rings embeds fully faithfully into the category of derived ones. Let us note though that in general, differently from the classical case, being a derived binomial ring for a derived commutative ring becomes a structure, and not a property.

There are some immediate benefits of our approach in defining
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}
: for example, by design, the forgetful functor
\DBinAlg\rightarrow\mathscr{D}(\mathbb Z)
commutes with sifted colimits
4
4

4

While the forgetful functor from
\DBinAlg
to
\mathbb E_{\infty}
-algebras in
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb Z)
in fact commutes with all small colimits.
. On the other hand, when restricted to a certain natural subcategory of
\Perf_{\mathbb Z}^{\geq 0}
,
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}
can be identified with the right Kan extension from
\mathrm{Lat}
, which altogether makes it possible to efficiently compute it in many cases. One also gets a derived binomial ring structure on the singular cohomology
C^{*}_{\mathrm{sing}}(X,\mathbb Z)
simply by considering the homotopy limit
\holim_{X}\mathbb Z
in
\DBinAlg
.

After setting up the definition, in
Section

3
we compute free derived binomial rings on classical abelian groups in a single cohomological degree. These guys are the building blocks of the category of derived binomial rings and cohomology of their underlying complexes can be seen as
“binomial cohomological operations”
. Since
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}\colon{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb Z)\rightarrow{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb Z)
commutes with filtered colimits it is in fact enough to understand
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(A[n])
for
A
being a lattice or a finite abelian group.

•

Classical case (
n=0
)
. The free binomial ring
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(\mathbb Z)=\mathrm{Bin}(\mathbb Z)
is described explicitly as the subring

\mathrm{Bin}(\mathbb Z)\simeq\mathbb Z[\tbinom{x}{n}]_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathbb Q[x]

generated by binomial coefficients

\tbinom{x}{n}\coloneqq\tfrac{x(x-1)\ldots(x-n+1)}{n!}\in\mathbb Q[x],

and, more functorially, as the ring of polynomial maps
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(\mathbb Z,\mathbb Z)
. One can also identify
\mathrm{Bin}(\mathbb Z)
with the ring of functions on the
“Hilbert additive group scheme”

\mathbb H
, defined as Cartier dual to the formal multiplicative group
\widehat{\mathbb G}_{m}
. In general, for a lattice
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
one has a canonical isomorphism
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(A)\simeq\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A^{\vee},\mathbb Z)
, where
A^{\vee}
is the dual lattice.

•

(Most of the) coconnective range
. Similarly to
Equation

1.1
in (the most of) coconnective range the free derived binomial algebra has a nice topological interpretation, namely (
3.6
) for
M\in\Perf^{\leq-1}_{\mathbb Z}
one has a natural equivalence:

\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(M^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\sim}C^{*}_{\mathrm{sing}}(K(M),\mathbb Z),

(1.2)

where
M^{\vee}\coloneqq R\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}(\mathbb Z)}(M,\mathbb Z)\in\Perf^{\geq 1}_{\mathbb Z}
is dual complex to
M
, and
K(M)\in{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{pc}}
is the (generalized) Eilenberg-MacLane space of
M
. In particular, for
M=\mathbb Z[n]
with
n\geq 1
we get an equivalence

\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(\mathbb Z[-n])\xrightarrow{\sim}C^{*}_{\mathrm{sing}}(K(\mathbb Z,n),\mathbb Z).

We give a different proof than in
[
Toë20
]
and
[
Hor22
]
, formally reducing to the case
M=\mathbb Z[-1]
, and then deforming
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(\mathbb Z[-1])
to the free divided power algebra
\mathbf{L}\Gamma(\mathbb Z[-1])
.

Note that the isomorphism
Equation

1.2
computes
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(A[-n])
where
A
is a lattice and
n\geq 1
or
A
is a finite abelian group and
n\geq 2
.

•

Strictly connective range
. It turns out that in the connective range all the interesting arithmetic of binomial algebras, that we saw in the coconnective case, collapses. More precisely, one gets the following rather surprising result (
3.27
): for
M\in\Perf^{\leq-1}_{\mathbb Z}
there is a natural equivalence:

\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(M)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(M\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q).

In particular, in this range the functor
M\mapsto\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}
only depends on the tensor product
M\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q
. Consequently, for a finite abelian group
A
,
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(A[n])
is trivial (isomorphic to
\mathbb Z
) for any
n\geq 1
; in fact, it is also trivial for
n=0
(
3.30
). For a lattice
A
, via the above isomorphism,
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(A[n])
can be identified with the homology
C_{*}^{\mathrm{sing}}(K(A\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q,n),\mathbb Z)
of the
n
-th Eilenberg-MacLane space of the rationalization
A\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q
.

•

A[-1]
for
A
finite
. The above results leave out the only case
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(A[-1])
for
A
finite abelian. Here we show that
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(A[-1])
is 1-cotruncated, namely
{\mathbf{L}^{>1}\mathrm{Bin}(A[-1])}\simeq 0
, and that the only interesting information is given by the functor
A\mapsto\mathbf{L}^{1}\mathrm{Bin}(A[-1])
, which we completely describe in
Section

3.5
. As an example of our computation, we can
(\mathbb Z/p)^{\times}
-equivariantly identify

\mathbf{L}^{1}\mathrm{Bin}(\mathbb Z/p[-1])\simeq K/\mathcal{O}_{K}

where
K=\mathbb Q_{p}(\mu_{p})
is the
p
-th cyclotomic extension and
(\mathbb Z/p)^{\times}
acts by multiplication on the left and via the Galois group
\Gal(K/\mathbb Q_{p})
on the right (see
3.44
).

Using the equivalence
Equation

1.2
in
Section

3.2
, similarly to
[
Hor22
]
, we establish a variant of integral homotopy type theory in our setting. Namely, we show that the functor

C^{*}_{\mathrm{sing}}(-,\mathbb Z)\colon{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{pc}}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\DBinAlg

is fully faithful when restricted to the full subcategory spanned by connected nilpotent spaces of finite type (see
3.14
). In particular, a connected space
X
(provided it is nilpotent of finite type) can be reconstructed from
C^{*}_{\mathrm{sing}}(X,\mathbb Z)
with its derived binomial ring structure: namely, there is a natural equivalence
X\simeq\Map_{\DBinAlg}(C^{*}_{\mathrm{sing}}(X,\mathbb Z),\mathbb Z)
(see
3.12
).

Remark 1.2
.

As we discuss in
Section

3.3
, the above isomorphism

\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(\mathbb Z[-n])\xrightarrow{\sim}C^{*}_{\mathrm{sing}}(K(\mathbb Z,n),\mathbb Z)
can be interpreted as comparison between the cohomology of the structure sheaf of two higher classifying stacks: namely,
K(\mathbb Z,n)
and
K(\mathbb H,n)
(where
\mathbb H\coloneqq\Spec(\mathrm{Bin}(\mathbb Z))
). The comparison map comes naturally from the homomorphism of group schemes

\iota\colon\underline{\mathbb Z}\rightarrow\mathbb H

that on the level of functions corresponds to the embedding
\mathrm{Bin}(\mathbb Z)\simeq\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(\mathbb Z,\mathbb Z)\subset\Map_{{\mathrm{Set}}}(\mathbb Z,\mathbb Z)
of polynomial functions into all.

This identification allows to interpret
C^{*}_{\mathrm{sing}}(K(\mathbb Z,n),\mathbb Z)
in terms of an object of more algebraic-geometric nature (namely, higher stack
K(\mathbb H,n)
).
One of the new structures that one gets from this, but which is not immediately seen topologically, is the deformation
5
5

5

Here
\mathbb G_{a}^{\sharp}\coloneqq\Spec(\Gamma(\mathbb Z))
is the Cartier dual to formal additive group
\widehat{\mathbb G}_{a}
. One gets a deformation
\mathbb H\rightsquigarrow\mathbb G_{a}^{\sharp}
from the canonical deformation of
\widehat{\mathbb G}_{m}
to its formal Lie algebra, which is
\widehat{\mathbb G}_{a}
.
from
K(\mathbb H,n)
to
K(\mathbb G_{a}^{\sharp},n)
, which, as we will show in the sequel, turns out to be trivial on the level of cohomology of the structure sheaf with respect to some (e.g.
\mathbb E_{n}
-algebra) structures.

1.4
Singular cohomology of log analytic spaces

The main new application of this paper is to integral singular cohomology of log analytic spaces over complex numbers.
Let us briefly recall what sort of structure this amounts to.

Definition 1.3

(
[
KN99
, Definition 1.1.1]
)
.

Recall that a log (complex) analytic space
(X,\mathcal{M})
is given by:

-

a complex analytic space
X
;

-

a sheaf of commutative monoids
\mathcal{M}
;

-

a homomorphism
\alpha\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
such that
\alpha^{-1}(\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{X})\simeq\mathcal{O}^{\times}_{X}
.

We will assume that our log analytic spaces are
fine and saturated (fs)
, this imposes some nice properties of the sheaf
\mathcal{M}
, as well as its group completion
\mathcal{M}^{\gr}
(see (
4.2
)). In particular, the stalks of
\overline{\mathcal{M}^{\gr}}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}^{\gr}/\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}
(which control the non-trivial part of log-structure) are free finitely generated abelian groups.

The classical approach to define log Betti (=singular) cohomology of a log analytic space, due to Kato and Nakayama
[
KN99
]
, is to associate to
(X,\mathcal{M})
a topological space
X^{\log}
(
4.3
) and define log Betti cohomology
R\Gamma^{\log}_{\mathrm{Betti}}(X,\mathbb Z)
as the singular cohomology
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{sing}}(X^{\log},\mathbb Z)
of
X^{\log}
. To give the reader a feeling of what
X^{\log}
looks like, let us just say for now that there is a continuous proper map
\pi\colon X^{\log}\rightarrow X
and that the fiber
\pi^{-1}(x)
over a point
x\in X
is given by a compact real torus with dimension given by the rank of the fiber of
\overline{\mathcal{M}^{\gr}}
at
x
.

However, one can also look for alternative, more
“direct”
, formulas for the log Betti cohomology that would only directly involve the analytic space
X
and the monoid
\mathcal{M}
, and would not use any auxiliary topological space. One variant of such construction was studied in great detail in
[
Shu22
]
in the case of
\mathbb Q
-coefficients. The key observation is that all necessary information in fact is captured by the 2-term exponential complex (
4.7
)

\eexp(\alpha)\coloneqq[\ \!\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{\exp_{\mathcal{M}}}\mathcal{M}^{\gr}\ \!]

of sheaves on
X
, where the map
\exp_{\mathcal{M}}
is given by the composition of the exponential map
\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}
and the composite embedding
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\gr}
. One can show that the complex
\eexp(\alpha)
gives an explicit model for
R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
((
4.36
)); moreover, even though the definition of
\eexp(\alpha)
uses the analytic structure on
X
, in the case when
(X,\mathcal{M})
is the analytification of an fs log scheme, it is essentially algebraic in the sense that it comes as the Betti realization of a certain motivic sheaf (see
[
Shu22
]
for more details).

To compute
R\Gamma^{\log}_{\mathrm{Betti}}(X,\mathbb Z)
one can proceed in two steps: namely, first evaluate the pushforward
R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
and then compute its (derived) global sections after that. So the goal is to give a closed formula for the full derived pushforward
R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
in terms of its first truncation
R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}\simeq\eexp(\alpha)
.

Let us sketch the case of
\mathbb Q
-coefficients following
[
Shu22
]
and then indicate what needs to be changed to get the case of
\mathbb Z
-coefficients.
Denote by

\eexp(\alpha)Q:=\mathpzc{exp}(\alpha)\otimes\mathbb Q

the rationalization of
\eexp(\alpha)
. The identification
\ker(\exp_{\mathcal{M}})=\underline{\mathbb Z}
induces maps

\underline{\mathbb Z}\rightarrow\eexp(\alpha),\qquad\underline{\mathbb Q}\rightarrow\eexp(\alpha)_{\mathbb Q}

of complexes of sheaves on
X
.

Using the natural commutative algebra structure on
R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Q}
the map
\eexp(\alpha)Q\simeq R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Q}\rightarrow R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Q}
extends to a commutative algebra map

\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Q}^{*}(\eexp(\alpha)Q)\dasharrow R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Q}

from the free commutative
\mathbb Q
-algebra on
\eexp(\alpha)
. Moreover, its composition with the natural map
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Q}^{*}(\underline{\mathbb Q})\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Q}^{*}(\eexp(\alpha)Q)
factors through
R^{0}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Q}\simeq\underline{\mathbb Q}
via the unique map whose restriction to
\mathrm{Sym}^{1}(\underline{\mathbb Q})\simeq\underline{\mathbb Q}
gives the identity map
{\mathrm{id}}_{\underline{\mathbb Q}}\colon\underline{\mathbb Q}\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb Q}
. Altogether, this produces a map

\mathrm{Sym}^{\mathrm{coaug}}_{\mathbb Q}(\eexp(\alpha)Q)\dasharrow R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Q}

from the pushout
6
6

6

Or, in other words, from the tensor product
\mathrm{Sym}^{\mathrm{coaug}}Q(\eexp(\alpha)Q)\simeq\underline{\mathbb Q}\otimes_{\mathrm{Sym}^{*}_{\mathbb Q}(\underline{\mathbb Q})}\mathrm{Sym}^{*}_{\mathbb Q}(\eexp(\alpha)Q)
.

\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Sym}^{{\mathrm{coaug}}}_{\mathbb Q}(\eexp(\alpha)Q)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Sym}^{*}_{{\mathbb Q}}(\underline{\mathbb Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Sym}^{*}_{\mathbb Q}(\eexp(\alpha)Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

which induces an equvalence

\mathrm{Sym}^{\mathrm{coaug}}_{\mathbb Q}(\eexp(\alpha)Q)\simeq R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Q}.

(1.3)

Once the map is constructed, the proof of the latter equivalence is not hard and, using proper base change, ultimately reduces to the fact that the natural map

\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Q}^{*}(\mathbb Q[-1])\rightarrow R\Gamma_{\mathrm{sing}}(S^{1},\mathbb Q)

(1.4)

is an equivalence. Let us also note that the formula
Equation

1.3
was first observed by Steenbrink (see
[
Ste95
, Section 2, (2.8)]
and
[
PS08
, Section 11.2.6]
) for log structures associated with normal crossing divisors.

However, if we now try to follow the same strategy to get a formula for
\mathbb Z
-coefficients we immediately run into trouble. The problem is with the functor
\mathrm{Sym}
; even though there are different options for the commutative algebra context in which to consider
R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
as an algebra, the free algebras in all of them will be too large. For example, the free
\mathbb E_{\infty}
-algebra functor now has a lot of non-trivial negative cohomology groups and, even if we correct that by considering the free derived commutative algebra functor
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Sym}^{*}_{\mathbb Z}
defined by
[
Rak20
]
, the analogously defined
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Sym}^{\mathrm{coaug}}_{\mathbb Z}(\eexp(\alpha))
would still be too big. In the latter case the problem is that
Equation

1.4
does not work with
\mathbb Z
-coefficients: the natural map

\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z[-1])\rightarrow R\Gamma_{\mathrm{sing}}(S^{1},\mathbb Z)

is far from being an equivalence
7
7

7

For an explicit description of
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z[-1])
see
[
KP22
]
where one first must identify
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z[-1])
with
R\Gamma(B\mathbb G_{a},\mathcal{O})
.
, e.g.
H^{2}(\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z[-1]))
is not even a finitely generated abelian group.

Note now that
S^{1}\simeq K(\mathbb Z,1)
and by
Equation

1.2
the natural map

\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}(\mathbb Z[-1])\rightarrow R\Gamma_{\mathrm{sing}}(S^{1},\mathbb Z)

is
an equivalence. It thus might be natural to try to use the free binomial ring
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}
in place of the free derived commutative algebra monad
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}
.

More precisely, in
Section

4.2
, under some mild assumptions on the topological space
X
, we define a derived binomial monad
\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}{in}_{X}
on the
\infty
-category
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{hv}}(X,{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb Z))
of
{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb Z)
-valued sheaves on
X
. We identify sheaves of derived binomial rings with modules over
\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}{in}_{X}
, and for a (nice enough) map
\pi\colon Y\rightarrow X
of topological spaces we endow the pushforward
R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
with a natural module structure over
\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}{in}_{X}
. In
Section

4.4
we then show that the natural map

\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}{in}^{\mathrm{coaug}}_{X}(\eexp(\alpha))\dasharrow R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z},

induced by
\eexp(\alpha)\rightarrow R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
, is an equivalence. Here, similar to what we had before,
\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}{in}^{\mathrm{coaug}}_{X}(\eexp(\alpha))
is defined as the pushout

\textstyle{\underline{\mathbb Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}{in}_{X}^{\mathrm{coaug}}(\eexp(\alpha))}
\textstyle{\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}{in}_{X}(\underline{\mathbb Z})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ev_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}{in}_{X}(\eexp(\alpha)),\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the map
\ev_{1}\colon\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}{in}_{X}(\underline{\mathbb Z})\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb Z}
is induced by the binomial algebra structure on
\mathbb Z
. In fact, as we show in
Section

4.3
the functor
\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}{in}_{X}^{\mathrm{coaug}}
can be considered as the free derived binomial algebra, but in the undercategory
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{hv}}(X,{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb Z))_{\setminus\underline{\mathbb Z}}

As the result we get a formula

R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}\simeq\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}{in}^{\mathrm{coaug}}_{X}(\mathpzc{exp}(\alpha)).

(1.5)

Taking global sections also leads to an equivalence

R\Gamma^{\log}_{\mathrm{Betti}}(X,\mathbb Z)\simeq R\Gamma(X,\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}\mathrm{in}_{X}^{\mathrm{coaug}}(\eexp(\alpha)))

expressing
integral
log Betti cohomology directly in terms of
\eexp(\alpha)
, extending Steenbrink’s formula, as well as its generalization in
[
Shu22
]
, to the integral case.

1.5
Cosheaf of spaces corresponding to
\pi\colon X^{\log}\rightarrow X
and its algebraic approximation
X^{\log}_{\mathbb H}

In fact not only the log Betti cohomology of
X
can be expressed via
\mathpzc{exp}(\alpha)
using the functor
\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}\mathrm{in}_{X}^{\mathrm{coaug}}
, but also the essential homotopy-theoretic data underlying the map
\pi\colon X^{\log}\rightarrow X
: more precisely, the cosheaf of spaces that it naturally defines.

More precisely, let
[X^{\log},\pi]
denote the the
(\infty,1)
-cosheaf of spaces on
X
which sends
U
to the underlying homotopy type of the preimage
\pi^{-1}(U)\in{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{pc}}
. Our version of integral homotopy type theory (
3.12
) implies that the cosheaf
[X^{\log},\pi]
can be reconstructed from
\mathpzc{exp}(\alpha)
as the cosheaf of spaces

U\mapsto\Map_{\DBinAlg}(\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}\mathrm{in}_{X}^{\mathrm{coaug}}(\eexp(\alpha))(U),\mathbb Z)

(see
4.55
).

As we show in
Section

4.5
the cosheaf
[X^{\log},\pi]
in fact also enjoys some better properties than the actual map of topological spaces
\pi\colon X^{\log}\rightarrow X
: for example (
4.49
), one can make sense of
[X^{\log},\pi]
as a gerbe over cosheaf of abelian groups given by
(\overline{\mathcal{M}^{\gr}})^{\vee}
defined by sending
U
to
\overline{\mathcal{M}^{\gr}}(U)^{\vee}\coloneqq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Ab}}(\overline{\mathcal{M}^{\gr}}(U),\mathbb Z)
. Intuitively, one would like to say similarly that
X^{\log}
itself is a gerbe over
\overline{\mathcal{M}^{\gr}}
(or rather a torsor over
\overline{\mathcal{M}^{\gr}}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}S^{1}
, which from homotopical point of view is the same as
\overline{\mathcal{M}^{\gr}}[1]
), but we don’t know in what sense this could be made precise. The key tool which allows to interpret
[X^{\log},\pi]
as a gerbe is
4.52
giving a certain enhancement of proper base change theorem for locally semi-analytic maps (
4.51
).

The gerbe structure on
[X^{\log},\pi]
also allows us to geometrize the equivalence
Equation

1.5
, which we do in
Section

4.6
. Namely, one can embed the category of cosheaves of spaces on
X
into the category of cosheaves with values in higher stacks via the natural colimit-preserving functor
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{pc}}\rightarrow\Stk_{/\mathbb Z}
. The group scheme homomorphism
i\colon\underline{\mathbb Z}\rightarrow\mathbb H
extends to the map of
\Stk_{/\mathbb Z}
-valued cosheaves
(\overline{\mathcal{M}^{\gr}})^{\vee}\rightarrow(\overline{\mathcal{M}^{\gr}})^{\vee}\!\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb H
and we define the cosheaf of stacks
X^{\log}_{\mathbb H}
(which we propose to be the
“algebraic version”
of
\pi\colon X^{\log}\rightarrow X
) as the induced
(\overline{\mathcal{M}^{\gr}})^{\vee}\!\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb H
-gerbe from
[X^{\log},\pi]
via the above map. One has a natural map

i_{X}\colon[X^{\log},\pi]\rightarrow X^{\log}_{\mathbb H}

and one obtains the isomorphism
Equation

1.5
by applying the derived global sections
R\Gamma(-,\mathscr{O})
(
4.61
) to
i_{X}
.

Remark 1.4
.

The construction of the gerbe
X^{\log}_{\mathbb H}
is in fact more general and applies to maps
\pi\colon Y\rightarrow X
with the property that locally
\pi^{-1}(U)
is homotopy equivalent to a compact real torus. In the case when
\pi
is given by toric fibration, the nature of the corresponding cosheaf of stacks
Y_{\mathbb H}
is somewhat analogous to the Hodge-Tate gerbe
X^{\mathrm{HT}}
for a scheme over
\mathbb F_{p}
(see
[
BL22
]
).

Namely,
p_{\mathrm{HT}}\colon X^{\mathrm{HT}}\rightarrow X
also represents a cosheaf of stacks that is a gerbe over the PD-envelope
T_{X}^{\sharp}
of the tangent bundle, and the latter is Cartier dual to a a formal group given by the completion at 0 of the cotangent bundle. The trivialization of this gerbe implies the formality of the Frobenius pushforward of the de Rham complex
F_{X*}\Omega_{X,{\mathrm{dR}}}^{*}
. On the other hand, the first truncation
\tau^{\leq 1}F_{X*}\Omega_{X,{\mathrm{dR}}}^{*}
splits if and only if so does the gerbe
p_{DI}\colon X^{DI}\rightarrow X
of Frobenius-liftings, defined by Deligne-Illusie in
[
DI87
]
.

The cosheaf of stacks
Y_{\mathbb H}\rightarrow Y
is a gerbe over
(R^{1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z})^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb H
, which is a Cartier dual to a sheaf of formal groups
R^{1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\widehat{\mathbb G}_{m}
. Differently from Hodge-Tate stack, the gerbe
Y_{\mathbb H}
is trivialized once one splits
R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
; the formality of the whole pushforward
R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
is then controlled by the triviality of a certain deformation of cosheaves
(R^{1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z})^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb H\rightsquigarrow(R^{1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z})^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb G_{a}^{\sharp}
, after applying
R\Gamma(-,\mathscr{O})
.

Remark 1.5
.

Unfortunately, it does not seem to be feasible that there is a lift of the binomial monad to the motivic level that would allow to associate an
integral
motive to a log structure, similarly to the construction in
[
Shu22
]
in the
\mathbb Q
-coefficients case. The main reason that we went through some analysis of the cosheaves
[X^{\log},\pi]
and
X^{\log}_{\mathbb H}
is that we expect that their construction could have a motivic lift instead. We also hope that there should be a way to define the relative motivic cohomology of
X^{\log}_{\mathbb H}
over
X
even in the non
\mathbb A^{1}
-homotopy-invariant context (as in
[
AHI23
]
) with
\mathbb H
replaced by the Cartier dual to a certain motivic formal group law. We hope to proceed with the construction of these generalizations in the close future.

1.6
Related work

The starting point for our project was Toën’s work
[
Toë20
]
on Grothendieck’s schematization problem; we then realized that the key computation of singular cohomology of
K(\mathbb Z,n)
could be also proved in another way by using the derived binomial ring structure. During the active phase of our project, the work
[
Hor22
]
by Horel came out, which used a similar idea (though with another proof) for the latter computation and which also established integral homotopy type theory in the context of
cosimplicial
binomial rings. The main difference with our work is that we use the whole derived category
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb Z)
rather than the cosimplicial part and we also avoid a direct use of the language of model categories; considering applications to integral cohomology both approaches seem to work well. We should point out that the integral homotopy type theory was (essentially) already established by Ekedahl in
[
Eke02
]
, though the language of
\infty
-categories allows for an (arguably) cleaner theory and formulation of final results. We should also probably mention the beautiful work of Mandell
[
Man06
]
where he showed that two nilpotent spaces of finite type are weakly homotopy equivalent if and only if their singular
\mathbb Z
-cochains are isomorphic as
\mathbb E_{\infty}
-algebras; note however that fully-faithfulness of the functor
C^{*}_{\mathrm{sing}}(-,\mathbb Z)
breaks in this context. There is another recent approach to integral homotopy type theory
[
Yua23
]
by Yuan, which is much more technically involved; we don’t know what is the precise relation between his work and derived binomial rings, though both approaches model in a way the
“trivialization of Frobenii action”
.

A version of
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}
over
\mathbb Z_{p}
should be related to the
“free
\delta
-ring monad”

\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Sym}^{\delta}
that was defined in
[
Hol23
]
: more precisely,
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}
should be given by the maximal Frobenius-invariant quotient of
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Sym}^{\delta}
in a certain sense. In particular, any derived binomial ring over
\mathbb Z_{p}
should have an underlying structure of derived
\delta
-ring. Given the main result
[
Hol23
, Theorem 1.1.3]
of
loc.cit.
that realizes prismatic cohomology functor as a left adjoint of the reduction functor from derived
\delta
-rings to derived commutative rings, it would be interesting to also find a purely categorical interpretation of the étale comparison for the prismatic cohomology
[
BS19
, Theorem 1.8(4)]
in terms of the category of derived binomial algebras over
\mathbb Z_{p}
.

Passing to the subject of log analytic spaces we should of course mention the work
[
Ste95
]
of Steenbrink which discusses in some detail the case when the log structure is associated with a normal crossing divisor. We hope that our work can shed more light on section
[
Ste95
, (2.8)]
, where the integral part of the story is discussed very briefly. The complex Steenbrink writes down in
[
Ste95
, (2.8)]
can be naturally identified with
\mathbf{L}\Gamma^{n}_{\mathbb Z}(R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z})
; he shows that it compares with
R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
for
n\gg 0
after
-\otimes\mathbb Q
. Our work provides an integral version of his result for any fs log analytic space: namely the full pushforward
R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
is given by
\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}in_{X}^{\mathrm{coaug}}(R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z})
. Despite his claim in the very end of
[
Ste95
, (2.8)]
about the existence of a natural map
\mathbf{L}\Gamma^{n}_{\mathbb Z}(R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z})\rightarrow R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
before
-\otimes\mathbb Q
we do not immediately see it from the binomial perspective
8
8

8

Instead, we clearly see a map

\cofib\left(\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}in_{X}^{\leq n-1}(R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z})\xrightarrow{[1]-1}\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}in_{X}^{\leq n}(R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z})\right)\dashrightarrow\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}in_{X}^{\mathrm{coaug}}(R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}),

(here
[1]
is the class in
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq 1}(\mathbb Z)
corresponding to the image of 1 in
{\mathrm{id}}(\mathbb Z)
under the decomposition of functor
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq 1}
as
{\mathrm{id}}\oplus\underline{\mathbb Z}
)
which indeed becomes an equivalence after tensoring with
\mathbb Q
when
n\gg 0
. The sheaf
\mathbf{L}\Gamma^{n}_{\mathbb Z}(R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z})
can be identified with the similar cofiber, but with
\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}in_{X}
replaced by
\mathbf{L}\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}
. However, since the latter is the associated graded of the former it is not clear a priori what the natural map unless we assume that there is some splitting of
\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}in_{X}
as
\mathbf{L}\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}
.
. In any case, we propose that
\mathbf{L}\Gamma^{n}_{\mathbb Z}(R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z})
for
n\gg 0
might as well be simply replaced by the sheaf
\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}in_{X}^{\mathrm{coaug}}(R^{\leq 1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z})
since it directly computes the full pushforward
R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
. In the case of a simple normal crossings divisor it thus would be nice to give a description of the weight filtration on
R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
in terms of a suitable explicit model for
\mathbf{L}\mathcal{B}in^{\mathrm{coaug}}_{X}(R^{1}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z})
, similarly to
[
Ste95
, (2.8)]
.

Besides that, in
[
AO19
]
Achinger and Ogus in certain cases describe the differential
d_{2}
on the
E_{2}
-page of the Leray-Serre spectral sequence for
\pi\colon X^{\log}\rightarrow X
. It would be interesting to explain their computations through the binomial ring perspective: namely the truncations
R^{[i,i+1]}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}\coloneqq\tau_{\leq i+1}\tau_{\geq i}R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
, which control
d_{2}
, are described in terms of the functor
\mathrm{Bin}^{[i,i+1]}\coloneqq\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq i+1}\!\!/\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq i-1}
which is a fairly explicit extension of
\Gamma^{i+1}_{\mathbb Z}
by
\Gamma^{i}_{\mathbb Z}
, at least when restricted to classical abelian groups. We plan to investigate this relation in the close future. Also, in the sequel we are going to use the
“binomial”
interpretation of
R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
to prove some general formality results for
R\pi_{*}\underline{\mathbb Z}
in certain contexts: as discussed in
4.39
this is directly controlled by the deformation of functors from
\mathrm{Bin}
to
\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}
.

2
Derived binomial monad

Let
\mathrm{Ab}
denote the category of abelian groups and let
\CAlg\coloneqq\CAlg(\mathrm{Ab})
be the category of classical commutative rings. The forgetful functor
\CAlg\rightarrow\mathrm{Ab}
has a left adjoint given by the free commutative ring
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}\coloneqq\oplus_{i\geq 0}\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}^{i}
. The composition of
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}
with the forgetful functor then defines a monad on
\mathrm{Ab}
and the natural functor
9
9

9

Namely, given a commutative ring
A
we have a natural ring homomorphism
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A)\rightarrow A
, which is given by the identity map
{\mathrm{id}}_{A}
on
A\simeq\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}^{1}(A)
, and then extended by multiplicativity.

\CAlg\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}}(\mathrm{Ab})
to the category of modules over this monad is an equivalence: prescribing the commutative algebra structure on an abelian group
M
is equivalent to giving a map of abelian groups
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(M)\rightarrow M
that defines the module structure over the above monad.

The goal of this section is to define another monad on the category of abelian groups, namely the
binomial monad

\mathrm{Bin}
. Modules over
\mathrm{Bin}
are given by
binomial rings
: namely, torsion-free commutative rings that are closed under operations given by evaluation of binomial coefficients. We start by giving a careful account for the relation of
\mathrm{Bin}
to (additively) polynomial maps between abelian groups in
Section

2.1
; this allows to define a natural filtered monad
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq*}
on the category
\mathrm{Lat}
of finitely generated free abelian groups. In
Section

2.2
we define the classical binomial monad
\mathrm{Bin}
on the category
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
of torsion free abelian groups and compare modules over it with classical binomial rings, considered e.g. in
[
Ell06
]
. In
Section

2.3
we recall the necessary results of
[
Rak20
]
which in
Section

2.4
allow us to construct a non-abelian derived extension
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}
of
\mathrm{Bin}
to the whole derived category
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb Z)
; here we crucially use that functors
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}
are polynomial. We define
derived binomial rings
as modules over
\mathbf{L}\mathrm{Bin}
in
\mathscr{D}(\mathbb Z)
. Besides that in
Section

2.4
we study some basic properties of derived binomial rings, relation to other derived algebraic contexts (e.g. derived commutative rings, or
\mathbb E_{\infty}
-rings) and finally give some examples, including most importantly the singular cohomology
C^{*}_{\mathrm{sing}}(X,\mathbb Z)
of any space
X\in{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{pc}}
(
2.58
).

2.1
Polynomial maps

Let
\mathrm{Lat}\subset\mathrm{Ab}
be the subcategory of free finitely generated abelian groups (=lattices). Let
L\in\mathrm{Lat}
; then
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(L)
naturally embeds into the ring
\Map(L^{\vee},\mathbb Z)
of
all

\mathbb Z
-valued functions on the dual lattice
L^{\vee}\coloneqq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(L,\mathbb Z)
. However, there are more functions on
L^{\vee}
that are polynomial in the following weaker sense.

Definition 2.1

(Polynomial maps)
.

Let
A,B
be abelian groups. By induction on
n
we can define what it means for a map
f\colon A\rightarrow B
(as sets) to be
polynomial of degree
\leq n
. Namely

•

f
is polynomial of degree
\leq-1
if
f
is identically 0;

•

f
is polynomial of degree
\leq n
if for each
a\in A
the map
D_{a}f\colon A\rightarrow B
defined as

(D_{a}f)(a^{\prime})=f(a+a^{\prime})-f(a^{\prime})

is polynomial of degree
\leq n-1
.
A map
f
is called
polynomial
if it is polynomial of some degree
\leq n
.

We denote
\Map_{\leq n}(A,B)
and
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,B)=\colim\Map_{\leq n}(A,B)
the sets of polynomial maps of degree
\leq n
and all polynomial maps from
A
to
B
correspondingly.

Remark 2.2
.

The following properties are straightforward to check from the definition:

•

f\colon A\rightarrow B
belongs to
\Map_{\leq n}(A,B)
if and only if for any
n+1
elements
a_{0},\ldots,a_{n}\in A

(D_{a_{n}}\ldots D_{a_{0}}f)\equiv 0.

•

If
f\in\Map_{\leq n}(A,B)
and
g\in\Map_{\leq m}(A,B)
then
f+g\in\Map_{\leq\max(n,m)}(A,B)
. In particular each
\Map_{\leq n}(A,B)
is an abelian group.

•

If
B
has a ring structure then for
f\in\Map_{\leq n}(A,B)
and
g\in\Map_{\leq m}(A,B)
their product
f\cdot g
lies in
\Map_{\leq n+m}(A,B)
.

•

If
f\in\Map_{\leq n}(A,B)
and
g\in\Map_{\leq m}(B,C)
then their composition
g\circ f
lies in
\Map_{\leq nm}(A,C)
. This gives a natural map of sets

\circ\colon\Map_{\leq m}(B,C)\times\Map_{\leq n}(A,B)\rightarrow\Map_{\leq nm}(A,C).

(2.1)

In particular, if
B=\mathbb Z
with its unique commutative ring structure, we get that
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)
has a natural structure of commutative ring.

We will be primarily interested in the case
B=\mathbb Z
.

Example 2.3
.

Let us explicitly describe
\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)
in low degrees:

1.

\Map_{\leq 0}(A,B)
is canonically identified with
B
, via the map
f\mapsto f(0)
. Indeed, the condition
f(a+a^{\prime})-f(a^{\prime})=0
implies that
f(a)=f(0)
for any
a\in A
. So
f\equiv{f(0)}
is constant.

2.

\Map_{\leq 1}(A,B)
canonically splits as
B\oplus\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(A,B)
via the map
f\mapsto(f(0),f-{f(0)})
. Indeed, to see that the map is well-defined, note that by part 1 we have that
D_{a}f
is a constant function for any
a\in A
. We also have
D_{a}f(0)=f(a+0)-f(0)=f(a)-f(0)
. Thus, if we replace
f
by
f^{\prime}=f-f(0)
we will get
D_{a}(f^{\prime})
is a constant function given by
f(a)
. Then we get
f^{\prime}(a)=(D_{a}f)(a^{\prime})=f^{\prime}(a+a^{\prime})-f^{\prime}(a^{\prime})
for any
a,a^{\prime}\in A
and so
f^{\prime}
is
\mathbb Z
-linear.
It is also straightforward to check that
\mathbb Z
-linear maps
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(A,B)
are polynomial of degree less or equal to 1. Indeed, for any
a\in A
and
\ell\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(A,B)
we have that
D_{a}\ell
is the constant function given by
\ell(a)
.

3.

In the case
B=\mathbb Z
the embedding
A^{\vee}\coloneqq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(A,\mathbb Z)\subset\Map_{\leq 1}(A,\mathbb Z)
extends to an algebra homomorphism

\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\rightarrow\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)

that sends a polynomial in
A^{\vee}
to the corresponding function on
A
that it defines. Note that this maps restricts to
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}^{\leq n}(A^{\vee})\rightarrow\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)
for every
n\geq 0
. Similarly, if
B=\mathbb Q
we get a map

\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\otimes\mathbb Q\simeq\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Q}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(A,\mathbb Q))\rightarrow\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Q).

Construction 2.4

(Hopf algebra
\mathbb Z[A]
)
.

Consider the functor
\mathbb Z[-]\colon{\mathrm{Set}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ab}
sending a set
I
to the free abelian group
\mathbb Z[I]
. Functor
\mathbb Z[-]
has a natural symmetric structure: one can naturally identify
\mathbb Z[S_{1}\times S_{2}]
with
\mathbb Z[S_{1}]\otimes\mathbb Z[S_{2}]
via the unique
\mathbb Z
-linear map that sends
[(s_{1},s_{2})]
to
[s_{1}]\otimes[s_{2}]
.

Any abelian group
A\in\mathrm{Ab}
gives a Hopf algebra in
{\mathrm{Set}}
: the addition map
+\colon A\times A\rightarrow A
gives multiplication,
0\rightarrow A
the unit map, the diagonal map
\Delta\colon A\rightarrow A\times A
gives comultiplication with counit
A\rightarrow 0
and, finally, the antipode
A\rightarrow A
is given by multiplication by
-1
. This endows
\mathbb Z[A]
with the natural Hopf algebra structure. More explicitly, the induced multiplication is defined by
[a_{1}]\cdot[a_{2}]=[a_{1}+a_{2}]
, and the comultiplication is given by
\Delta([a])=[a]\otimes[a]
; we also have
1=[0]
. The augmentation ideal
10
10

10

By definition this is the kernel of the counit map
\mathbb Z[A]\rightarrow\mathbb Z\simeq\mathbb Z[0]
.

I_{A}\subset\mathbb Z[A]
is generated by elements
([a]-1)_{a\in A}
. The group
I_{A}/I_{A}^{2}
is naturally identified with
A
via the map
a\mapsto[a]-1
.

Note that in the case
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
is free and finitely generated, the corresponding affine scheme
\Spec\mathbb Z[A]
is a torus, more precisely
\mathbb G_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A^{\vee}
. Since the latter is smooth over
\mathbb Z
we can identify
I_{A}^{n}/I_{A}^{n+1}
with
\mathrm{Sym}^{n}_{\mathbb Z}(I_{A}/I_{A}^{2})\simeq\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}^{n}(A)
.

As usual, the
I_{A}
-adic completion
\mathbb Z[A]^{\wedge}_{I_{A}}
defines a topological Hopf algebra; the corresponding formal group scheme is identified with the formal completion
\widehat{\mathbb G}_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A^{\vee}
. The completed associated graded
\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}I_{A}^{n}/I_{A}^{n+1}
with respect to
I_{A}
-adic filtration is identified with
\mathbb Z[[A]]
; moreover, since for any Hopf algebra for
x\in I_{A}
one has
\Delta(x)=x\otimes 1+1\otimes x\pmod{I_{A}\otimes I_{A}}
, one has
\Delta\colon a\mapsto[a]\otimes 1+1\otimes[a]\in\Spf[[A]]\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}\Spf[[A]]
. In other words, the corresponding associated graded formal group scheme is identified with the additive group
\widehat{\mathbb G}_{a}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A^{\vee}
.

Following
[
Pas68
]
one can naturally describe
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)
in terms of of the group algebra
\mathbb Z[A]
(or rather its topological dual):

Construction 2.5

(Description of
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,B)
via the group algebra
\mathbb Z[A]
)
.

By the universal property of
\mathbb Z[A]
we can extend any set-theoretic map
f\colon A\rightarrow B
to an abelian group homomorphism
\tilde{f}\colon\mathbb Z[A]\rightarrow B
. We claim that
f
is polynomial of degree
\leq n
if and only if
f
vanishes on
I_{A}^{n+1}
. Indeed, for any
x\in A
one has

(D_{a_{n}}\ldots D_{a_{0}}f)(x)=\tilde{f}(([a_{0}]-1)([a_{1}]-1)\ldots([a_{n}]-1)[x])

and so the condition
(D_{a_{n}}\ldots D_{a_{0}}f)=0
is equivalent to
\tilde{f}(([a_{0}]-1)([a_{1}]-1)\ldots([a_{n}]-1)x)=0
for all
x\in A
. This gives a description of
\Map_{\leq n}(A,B)
as
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{n+1},B)
and
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,B)\simeq\colim_{n}\Map_{\leq n}(A,B)
as

\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,B)\simeq\colim_{n}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{n+1},B)

In the case
B=\mathbb Z
this gives natural identifications

\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)\simeq(\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{n+1})^{\vee}
and

\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)\simeq\colim_{n}\ (\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{n+1})^{\vee}.

(2.2)

In other words,
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)
is naturally identified with distributions
11
11

11

See for example
[
Jan07
, Section 7]
.
at 0 of
\Spec\mathbb Z[A]
. In the case
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
, the algebra
\mathbb Z[A]
is smooth over
\mathbb Z
and the Hopf algebra structure on
\mathbb Z[A]
induces a Hopf algebra structure on
\colim_{n}(\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{n+1})^{\vee}
(see
[
Jan07
, Section 7.7]
). By
2.4
the corresponding flat affine group scheme be identified more explicitly with the distributions
\mathrm{Dist}(\mathbb G_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A^{\vee})\simeq\mathrm{Dist}(\mathbb G_{m})\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A
or, equivalently, the Cartier dual of
\widehat{\mathbb G}_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A^{\vee}
.

Remark 2.6
.

Note that unit and counit maps give a splitting
\mathbb Z[A]\simeq\mathbb Z\oplus I_{A}
, functorially in
A
. Under the isomorphism
A\rightarrow I_{A}/I_{A}^{2}
sending
a
to
[a]-1
this induces a splitting of
\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{2}
as
\mathbb Z\oplus A
. Then the resulting identification

\Map_{\leq 1}(A,B)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{2},B)\simeq B\oplus\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(A,B)

is exactly the one we constructed in
2.3
(
2
); indeed, a map
f
is being send to a pair
(f(0),f-f(0))
, where
f-f(0)\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(A,B)
.

Remark 2.7

(
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)
is a symmetric monoidal functor)
.

Let
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
. Since the group scheme
\mathbb G_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A
is smooth, and
\mathbb G_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}(A_{1}\oplus A_{2})\simeq(\mathbb G_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A_{1})\times(\mathbb G_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A_{2})
, one has (see e.g.
[
Jan07
, Section 7.4,(2)]
)

\mathrm{Dist}(\mathbb G_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}(A_{1}\oplus A_{2}))\simeq\mathrm{Dist}(\mathbb G_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A_{1})\times\mathrm{Dist}(\mathbb G_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A_{2}).

On the level of functions (applying the above to
A_{1}^{\vee},A_{2}^{\vee}\in\mathrm{Lat}
) this is saying that the natural map

\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A_{1},\mathbb Z)\otimes\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A_{2},\mathbb Z)\rightarrow\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A_{1}\oplus A_{2},\mathbb Z)

induced by multiplication of pull-backs under projections
A_{1}\leftarrow A_{1}\oplus A_{2}\rightarrow A_{2}
is an isomorphism. This shows that the functor
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(-,\mathbb Z)\colon\mathrm{Lat}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ab}
is symmetric monoidal, and one can identify the Hopf algebra structure on
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)
for
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
with the one induced by the canonical Hopf algebra structure on
A
as an object of
\mathrm{Lat}^{\mathrm{op}}
via the monoidal structure given by
\oplus
(where multiplication is given by the (opposite to) the diagonal
\Delta\colon A\rightarrow A\oplus A
, the comultiplication by the addition
A\oplus A\rightarrow A
, et.c.).

Remark 2.8

(Associated graded of
\Map_{\leq*}(A,\mathbb Z)
)
.

Let
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
. The Hopf algebra
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)
has a filtration given by
\Map_{\leq*}(A,\mathbb Z)
. Identifying
\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)
with
(\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{n+1})^{\vee}
we see that the associated graded Hopf algebra
\gr_{*}\Map_{\leq*}(A,\mathbb Z)
is Cartier dual to the completed associated graded
\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}I_{A}^{n}/I_{A}^{n+1}
. By
2.4
the corresponding formal group scheme is identified with
\widehat{\mathbb G}_{a}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A^{\vee}
, and the Cartier dual is given by
\mathrm{Dist}(\mathbb G_{a}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A^{\vee})\simeq\mathrm{Dist}(\mathbb G_{a})\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A
. The group scheme
\mathrm{Dist}(\mathbb G_{a})
is often denoted
\mathbb G_{a}^{\sharp}
. The Hopf algebra
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb G_{a}^{\sharp})
is given by the free divided power algebra
\Gamma^{*}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z)=\mathbb Z[\frac{x^{n}}{n!}]_{n\geq 1}
, and more generally there is a natural isomorphism
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb G_{a}^{\sharp}\otimes A)\simeq\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}^{*}(A^{\vee})
.

Summarizing, we get an isomorphism of Hopf algebras

\gr_{*}\Map_{\leq*}(A,\mathbb Z)\simeq\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}^{*}(A^{\vee}).

Remark 2.9

(Map
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\rightarrow\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)
)
.

By
2.3
(3) we have a natural filtered map
\mathrm{Sym}^{\leq*}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\rightarrow\Map_{\leq*}(A,\mathbb Z)
. Since it is functorial in
A
, it is a Hopf algebra homomorphism (indeed, both Hopf algebra structures is induced by the natural Hopf algebra structure on
A^{\vee}
as an object of
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{op}}
with the monoidal structure given by
\oplus
). By passing to the associated graded we get a Hopf algebra map

\mathrm{Sym}^{*}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\rightarrow\Gamma^{*}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee}).

By the universal property of
\mathrm{Sym}^{*}_{\mathbb Z}
the above map is uniquely extended from its restriction to
A^{\vee}\simeq\mathrm{Sym}^{1}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})
, which maps isomorphically to
A^{\vee}\simeq\Gamma^{1}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})
. In degree
n
, the resulting map

\mathrm{Sym}^{n}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\rightarrow\Gamma^{n}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})
is identified with the
norm map

\mathrm{Sym}^{n}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\simeq(A^{\vee}\otimes\ldots\otimes A^{\vee})_{\Sigma_{n}}\rightarrow(A^{\vee}\otimes\ldots\otimes A^{\vee})^{\Sigma_{n}}\simeq\Gamma^{n}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})
from
\Sigma_{n}
-coinvariants to
\Sigma_{n}
-invariants: namely one lifts an element
x\in((A^{\vee})^{\otimes n})_{\Sigma_{n}}
to an element
\widetilde{x}\in(A^{\vee})^{\otimes n}
and maps it to the average
\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}}\sigma(\widetilde{x})\in((A^{\vee})^{\otimes n})^{\Sigma_{n}}
. In the case
A^{\vee}\simeq\mathbb Z
the above map is given by the natural embedding
\mathbb Z[x]\rightarrow\mathbb Z[\frac{x^{n}}{n!}]_{n\geq 1}
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
. The natural map

\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\otimes\mathbb Q\simeq\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Q}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(A,\mathbb Q))\rightarrow\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Q)

from
2.3
(3) is an isomorphism. In other words, any
\mathbb Q
-valued polynomial function in the sense of
2.1
is a polynomial in linear functions.

Proof.

By construction, the above map is filtered:
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Q}^{\leq n}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(A,\mathbb Q))
lands in
\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Q)
. Both filtrations are exhaustive, so it would be enough to show that the associated graded map is an isomorphism. Note that

\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Q)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{n+1},\mathbb Q)\simeq(\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{n+1})^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb Q\simeq\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)\otimes\mathbb Q,

since
\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{n+1}
is a finitely generated abelian group. By taking colimit we also get an identification
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Q)\simeq\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q
. By similar reasoning
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(A,\mathbb Q)\simeq A^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb Q
, and so the map in question is identified with the map
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\rightarrow\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)
tensor
\mathbb Q
. The map on the associated graded is then identified with

\mathrm{Sym}^{*}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\otimes\mathbb Q\rightarrow\Gamma^{*}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\otimes{\mathbb Q},

which is an isomorphism (in each degree
n
the inverse to the norm map
((A^{\vee})^{\otimes n})_{\Sigma_{n}}\rightarrow((A^{\vee})^{\otimes n})^{\Sigma_{n}}
is given by the natural projection
((A^{\vee})^{\otimes n})^{\Sigma_{n}}\rightarrow((A^{\vee})^{\otimes n})_{\Sigma_{n}}
divided by
n!=|\Sigma_{n}|
).
∎

Remark 2.11

(
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)
as integer-valued polynomials)
.

By
2.10
we get that any polynomial
\mathbb Q
-valued function on
A
is a polynomial in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(A,\mathbb Q)
(=linear functions) with rational coefficients. This way,
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)\subset\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Q)
is identified with the subring of those
\mathbb Q
-polynomials in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(A,\mathbb Q)
that take integer values on
A
.

Let us specialize to the case
A=\mathbb Z
. The explicit description of integer-valued rational polynomials in one variable has been established more than a century ago by Polya in
[
Pól15
]
. Namely, let
x\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z,\mathbb Z)\simeq\mathbb Z^{\vee}
be the identity map. Then
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(\mathbb Z,\mathbb Z)
is identified with the subring
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z^{\vee})\otimes\mathbb Q\simeq\mathbb Q[x]
generated over
\mathbb Z
by
“binomial coefficients”

{x\choose n}\coloneqq\frac{x(x-1)\ldots(x-n+1)}{n!}\in\mathbb Q[x].

The corresponding function
\mathbb Z\rightarrow\mathbb Z
that it defines is given by the evaluation of the above binomial coefficient.

More generally,
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z)\subset\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Q)\simeq\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\otimes\mathbb Q
is identified with the
\mathbb Z
-subalgebra generated by the binomial coefficients
{[\ell]\choose n}\in\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})\otimes\mathbb Q
where
[\ell]\in\mathrm{Sym}^{1}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})
is the element which corresponds to a linear function
\ell\in A^{\vee}
via the isomorphism
A^{\vee}\simeq\mathrm{Sym}^{1}_{\mathbb Z}(A^{\vee})
.

Remark 2.12

(Comparison with
\mathrm{Dist}(\mathbb G_{m})
)
.

Let us compare the identification
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(\mathbb Z,\mathbb Z)\simeq\mathbb Z[\binom{x}{n}]_{n\geq 1}
with description as algebra of distributions on
\mathbb G_{m}
. Let us identify
\mathbb Z[\mathbb Z]\simeq\mathbb Z[t,t^{-1}]
, where
[1]\mapsto t
; this way the augmentation ideal is identified with
(t-1)\subset\mathbb Z[t,t^{-1}]
. Then, following
2.6
and splitting
\mathbb Z[t,t^{-1}]/(t-1)^{2}
as
\mathbb Z\cdot 1\oplus\mathbb Z\cdot(t-1)
, we see that the identity function
x\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb Z}(\mathbb Z,\mathbb Z)
corresponds to a functional in
(\mathbb Z[t,t^{-1}]/(t-1)^{2})^{\vee}
that is 0 on 1 and is equal to 1 on
t-1
. Then (see e.g.
[
Jan07
, Section 7.8]
, there
x
is denoted by
\delta_{1}
) one can identify
\binom{x}{n}
with the functional in
(\mathbb Z[t,t^{-1}]/(t-1)^{n+1})^{\vee}
that is zero on
1,t-1,\ldots,(t-1)^{n-1}
and is 1 on
(t-1)^{n}
. So, one can think of
\{\binom{x}{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
(here we put
\binom{x}{0}\coloneqq 1
) as a dual basis to
\{(t-1)^{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
(the latter of course is not a basis of
\mathbb Z[t,t^{-1}]
, but is a topological basis of the completion
{\mathbb Z[t,t^{-1}]}^{\wedge}_{(t-1)}
).

Remark 2.13

(Hopf algebra structure on
\mathbb Z[\binom{x}{n}]_{n\geq 1}
)
.

Let us describe the Hopf algebra structure on
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(\mathbb Z,\mathbb Z)
in terms of identification with
\mathbb Z[\binom{x}{n}]_{n\geq 1}
. The comultiplication is induced by the addition map
\mathbb Z\oplus\mathbb Z\rightarrow\mathbb Z
, which dually corresponds to the diagonal embedding
\mathbb Z^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathbb Z^{\vee}\oplus\mathbb Z^{\vee}.
Thus
x={\mathrm{id}}_{\mathbb Z}\in\mathbb Z^{\vee}
is being sent to
(x,x)
. This means
\Delta\colon\mathbb Z[\tbinom{x}{n}]_{n\geq 1}\rightarrow\mathbb Z[\tbinom{x}{n}]_{n\geq 1}\otimes\mathbb Z[\tbinom{x}{n}]_{n\geq 1}
sends
x
to
x\otimes 1+1\otimes x
; it then extends uniquely to
\mathbb Z[\tbinom{x}{n}]_{n\geq 1}
as:

\Delta\left(\binom{x}{n}\right)=\sum_{i+j=n,i,j\geq 0}\binom{x}{i}\otimes\binom{x}{j}

The natural map
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}((\mathbb Z)^{\vee})\rightarrow\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(\mathbb Z,\mathbb Z)
from
2.3
(3) is a map of Hopf algebras that is given explicitly as the embedding

\mathbb Z[x]\rightarrow\mathbb Z[\tbinom{x}{n}]_{n\geq 1}.

Notation 2.14
.

Let
\mathbb H\coloneqq\Spec(\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(\mathbb Z,\mathbb Z))\simeq\mathrm{Dist}(\mathbb G_{m})
;
\mathbb H
is called the
“Hilbert additive group scheme”
in
[
Toë20
]
. Note that by the discussion in
2.5
for any
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
one has a natural identification
\Spec(\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A,\mathbb Z))\simeq\mathrm{Dist}(\mathbb G_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A)\simeq\mathbb H\otimes_{\mathbb Z}A^{\vee}
.

Remark 2.15
.

The homomorphism
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}((\mathbb Z)^{\vee})\rightarrow\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(\mathbb Z,\mathbb Z)
induces a map of affine group schemes
\mathbb H\rightarrow\mathbb G_{a}
over
\Spec\mathbb Z
. By (the proof of)
2.10
we get that it is an isomorphism over the generic fiber
\Spec\mathbb Q\subset\Spec\mathbb Z
. However, modulo each prime
p
this map is very far from being one. Indeed, over
\mathbb F_{p}
one has an identification
\widehat{\mathbb G}_{m}\simeq\colim_{n}\mu_{p^{n}}
, so
\mathbb H_{\mathbb F_{p}}\simeq\underline{\mathbb Z_{p}}\coloneqq\lim_{n}\underline{\mathbb Z/p^{n}}
, and the map
\mathbb H_{\mathbb F_{p}}\rightarrow\mathbb G_{a,\mathbb F_{p}}
is given by the composition

\underline{\mathbb Z_{p}}\twoheadrightarrow\underline{\mathbb Z/p}\rightarrow\mathbb G_{a,\mathbb F_{p}},

where the first map is the natural projection, and the second is the embedding of the constant group scheme given by
\mathbb F_{p}
-points of
\mathbb G_{a,\mathbb F_{p}}
.

Let us finally record the last piece of data that we will need to define the binomial monad:

Construction 2.16

(Canonical map
\ev_{A}\colon A\mapsto\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)^{\vee}
)
.

For any
a\in A
the association
f\mapsto f(a)
defines a linear function on
\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)
. We claim that the corresponding map of sets
A\rightarrow\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)^{\vee}
is in fact polynomial of degree
\leq n
. Indeed, via the identification
\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)\simeq(\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{n+1})^{\vee}
in
2.5
the
\mathbb Z
-linearization
\mathbb Z[A]\rightarrow\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)^{\vee}
factors through the natural linear evaluation map
\ev\colon\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{n+1}\rightarrow((\mathbb Z[A]/I_{A}^{n+1})^{\vee})^{\vee}
.

This gives a canonical class
\ev_{A}\in\Map_{\leq n}(A,\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)^{\vee})
. By
2.2
(3) there is a natural composition map

\Map_{\leq m}(\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)^{\vee},\mathbb Z)\times\Map_{\leq n}(A,\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)^{\vee})\rightarrow\Map_{\leq nm}(A,\mathbb Z),

from which, plugging
\ev_{A}
in the second variable we get natural in
A
maps

\Map_{\leq m}(\Map_{\leq n}(A,\mathbb Z)^{\vee},\mathbb Z)\rightarrow\Map_{\leq nm}(A,\mathbb Z).

Note that if we plug
A^{\vee}
the left and right hand sides become more symmetric:

\Map_{\leq m}(\Map_{\leq n}(A^{\vee},\mathbb Z)^{\vee},\mathbb Z)\rightarrow\Map_{\leq nm}(A^{\vee},\mathbb Z).

(2.3)

2.2
Classical binomial monad

We now can define free classical binomial algebras.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
. We define the
free binomial algebra

\mathrm{Bin}(A)
to be
\Map_{\mathrm{poly}}(A^{\vee},\mathbb Z)
. The filtration of
\mathrm{Bin}(A)
by
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}(A)\coloneqq\Map_{\leq n}(A^{\vee},\mathbb Z)
endows it with the filtered algebra structure.

We will denote by
\mathrm{Bin}(A)
both the corresponding algebra and the underlying abelian group, hopefully not leading to any confusion.

Remark 2.18
.

Let
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
. Let us resummarize some properties of polynomial maps that we have established in previous section in terms of
\mathrm{Bin}
:

1.

The filtration on
\mathrm{Bin}(A)
given by
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq*}(A)
has associated graded
\Gamma^{*}_{\mathbb Z}(A)
, functorially in
A
(
2.8
).

2.

One has
\mathrm{Bin}(0)\simeq\mathbb Z
. Maps
0\rightarrow A\rightarrow 0
split
\mathrm{Bin}(A)
as
\mathbb Z\oplus\overline{\mathrm{Bin}}(A)
(the latter is the definition of
\overline{\mathrm{Bin}}(A)
). In particular,
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq 1}(A)
splits as
\mathbb Z\oplus A
(see
2.6
).

3.

There is a natural in
A
filtered ring homomorphism
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}^{\leq*}(A)\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq*}(A)
(see
2.3
(3)). The induced map

\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}^{i}(A)\rightarrow\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}^{i}(A)

on the
i
-th graded piece is the norm map
(A^{\otimes i})_{\Sigma_{i}}\rightarrow(A^{\otimes i})^{\Sigma_{i}}
(see
2.9
).

4.

\mathrm{Bin}(A)
naturally embeds into
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q
, and the image can be identified with the
\mathbb Z
-subalgebra of
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q
generated by binomial coefficients

\binom{[a]}{n}=\frac{[a]([a]-1)\ldots([a]-n+1)}{n!}\in\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q,

where
[a]
is the image of
a\in A
under the identification
A\simeq\mathrm{Sym}^{1}_{\mathbb Z}(A)
(see
2.11
).

5.

Functor
\mathrm{Bin}
is symmetric monoidal: the natural filtered ring homomorphism

\mathrm{Bin}(A_{1})\otimes\mathrm{Bin}(A_{2})\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}(A_{1}\oplus A_{2})

is an isomorphism (see
2.7
).

Unfortunately, functor
A\mapsto\mathrm{Bin}(A)
does not preserve
\mathrm{Lat}
, so there is no way it would define a monad on it. Nevertheless, if
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
then
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}(A)
is still a free finitely generated abelian group, and
Equation

2.3
provides us with the natural in
A
composition maps

\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq m}(\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}(A))\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq nm}(A).

Such data gives rise to a
filtered monad
in the sense of
[
Rak20
]
.

Let
\mathbf{\mathbb Z}^{\times}_{\geq 0}
denote the symmetric monoidal category corresponding to the poset of non-negative integers endowed with the monoidal structure given by multiplication. Given an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
we endow the category of endofunctors
\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{C})
with the monoidal structure given by composition (see
[
Lur17
, Section 4.7]
).

Definition 2.19

(
[
Rak20
, Definition 4.1.2]
)
.

A
filtered monad

\mathrm{T}^{\leq*}
on an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
is the data of a lax-monoidal functor

\mathrm{T}^{\leq*}\colon\mathbb Z^{\times}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{C}).

We denote by
\mathrm{T}^{\leq n}\colon\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
the endofunctor corresponding to
n\in\mathbb Z^{\times}_{\geq 0}
.

Note that a lax-monoidal functor between monoidal categories in the classical sense gives rise to a lax-monoidal functor in the
\infty
-categorical setting between the corresponding monoidal
\infty
-categories.

Example 2.20
.

The following give natural examples of filtered monads on
\mathrm{Lat}
:

1.

The natural transformations

\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}\circ\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq m}\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq nm}

given by
Equation

2.3
for any
n,m\in\mathbb Z^{\times}_{\geq 0}
and
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
endow the association
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq*}\colon n\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}\in\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathrm{Lat})
with the structure of a filtered monad.

2.

The natural transformation
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}\circ\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}
given by monad structure on
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}
restricts to transformations

\mathrm{Sym}^{\leq n}\circ\mathrm{Sym}^{\leq m}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}^{\leq nm}

that endow the association
\mathrm{Sym}^{\leq*}\colon n\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}^{\leq n}\in\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathrm{Lat})
with the structure of a filtered monad.

3.

The natural transformation
\mathrm{Sym}^{\leq n}(A)\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}(A)
(given by
2.3
(
3
) for
A^{\vee}
) agrees with the composition and defines a map of filtered monads

\mathrm{Sym}^{\leq*}\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq*}.

We will now show how filtered monad
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq*}
defines a classical binomial monad
\mathrm{Bin}
(considered, for example, in
[
Ell06
]
) on the category
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
of torsion-free abelian groups. Note that one can identify the category
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
of torsion-free abelian groups with the ind-completion
\Ind(\mathrm{Lat})
of
\mathrm{Lat}
.

Construction 2.21

(Binomial monad on
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
)
.

By the universal property of
\Ind(\mathrm{Lat})
, for any
\infty
-category
\mathscr{D}
that has filtered colimits, the restriction to
\mathrm{Lat}\subset\Ind(\mathrm{Lat})\simeq\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
induces an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathscr{F}\mathrm{un}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}},\mathscr{D})\simeq\mathscr{F}\mathrm{un}\!\left(\mathrm{Lat},\mathscr{D}\right),

(2.4)

where
\mathscr{F}\mathrm{un}_{\omega}
denotes the category of filtered colimit preserving functors. The inverse functor of this equivalence is given by left Kan extension along
\mathrm{Lat}\subset\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
. In particular, given
F\in\mathscr{F}\mathrm{un}\!\left(\mathrm{Lat},\mathscr{D}\right)
, the value of the corresponding functor
F\in\mathscr{F}\mathrm{un}_{\omega}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}},\mathscr{D})
on a torsion-free abelian group
M
can be computed as
\colim_{i}F(M_{i})
where
M=\colim M_{i}
is any presentation of
M
as a filtered colimit of
M_{i}\in\mathrm{Lat}
. By plugging
\mathscr{D}\coloneqq\Ind(\mathrm{Lat})
, the equivalence (
2.4
) restricts to a monoidal equivalence

\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}_{\omega}^{\mathrm{Lat}}(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}})\simeq\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathrm{Lat}),

where
\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}_{\omega}^{\mathrm{Lat}}
denotes the category of filtered colimit preserving endofunctors that also preserve the subcategory
\mathrm{Lat}\subset\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
.
In particular, a filtered monad
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq*}\colon\mathbb Z^{\times}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathrm{Lat})
gives rise to a filtered monad on
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
, which we will continue to denote the same way. The corresponding endofunctors
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}\colon\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
commute with filtered colimits and this way

\mathrm{Bin}\coloneqq\colim_{n\in\mathbb Z^{\times}_{\geq 0}}\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}\colon\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}

has a natural monad structure. Namely, the multiplication
\mathrm{Bin}\circ\mathrm{Bin}\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}
is induced by the transformation

\colim_{n\in\mathbb Z^{\times}_{\geq 0}}\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}\circ\colim_{m\in\mathbb Z^{\times}_{\geq 0}}\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq m}\rightarrow\colim_{m,n\in\mathbb Z^{\times}_{\geq 0}}\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq nm}\simeq\colim_{s\in\mathbb Z^{\times}_{\geq 0}}\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq s}.

Remark 2.22
.

Since for any
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
the value of
\mathrm{Bin}(A)
(defined as in
2.21
) is obtained as a colimit of
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq n}(A)
over
n
, one sees that it coincides with (the underlying abelian group) of
\mathrm{Bin}(A)
defined as in
2.17
. For general
A\in\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
one can compute
\mathrm{Bin}(A)
as
\colim_{i}\mathrm{Bin}(A_{i})
of all submodules
A_{i}\subset A
such that
A_{i}\in\mathrm{Lat}
.

Remark 2.23
.

Note that
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}\colon\mathrm{Ab}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ab}
restricts to a monad on
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
and that it is filtered colimit commuting: indeed,
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}\simeq\oplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Sym}^{i}_{\mathbb Z}
, where
\mathrm{Sym}^{i}_{\mathbb Z}(A)=(A^{\otimes i})_{\Sigma_{i}}
and both tensor power and coinvariants commute with filtered colimits. Thus
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}=\colim_{i}\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}^{\leq i}\colon\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
is obtained as left Kan extension from
\mathrm{Lat}
and is realized as colimit of the filtered monad
\mathrm{Sym}^{\leq*}_{\mathbb Z}
(
2.20
(
2
)) on
\mathrm{Lat}
. In particular the transformation
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}^{\leq*}\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq*}
from
2.20
(
3
) induces a map

\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}_{\mathbb Z}

of monads on
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
.

Remark 2.24
.

Functor
\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}\coloneqq\oplus_{i}\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}^{i}\colon\mathrm{Ab}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ab}
with
\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}^{i}(A)=(A^{\otimes i})^{\Sigma_{i}}
also preserves the subcategory
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
and commutes with filtered colimits: indeed,
\Sigma_{i}
-invariants are computed by finite limit, which commutes with filtered colimits. Thus the restriction of
\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}
to
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
is also left Kan extended from
\mathrm{Lat}
.

Functor
\mathrm{Bin}
comes with a natural exhaustive filtration
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq*}
. When restricted to
\mathrm{Lat}
, the associated graded
\oplus_{i}\gr_{i}\mathrm{Bin}_{|\mathrm{Lat}}
is naturally identified with
\oplus_{i}(\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}^{i})_{|\mathrm{Lat}}
(
2.18
), thus left Kan extending with get a similar identification
\oplus_{i}\gr_{i}\mathrm{Bin}\simeq\oplus_{i}\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}^{i}
as endofunctors of
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
.

Definition 2.25
.

A
classical binomial ring

A
is a module over the binomial monad
\mathrm{Bin}
in
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
. In other words, it is a torsion-free abelian group
A
together with a map
\mathrm{Bin}(A)\rightarrow A
that is compatible with the monad structure on
\mathrm{Bin}
. We will denote by
\BinAlg
the category of binomial rings.

Remark 2.26
.

The map of monads
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}
induces a functor
\BinAlg\rightarrow\CAlg
; in particular any classical binomial ring has an underlying classical ring structure.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
V\in\Vect_{\mathbb Q}\subset\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
be a
\mathbb Q
-vector space. Then the map

\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(V)\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}(V)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The above map is filtered with respect to exhaustive filtrations given by
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}^{\leq*}(V)
and
\mathrm{Bin}^{\leq*}(V)
. Thus it would be enough to show that the map on the associated graded pieces is an isomorphism. There we have the norm map
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}^{i}(V)\rightarrow\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}^{i}(V)
, which is an isomorphism since
V
is a
\mathbb Q
-vector space.
∎

Remark 2.28
.

More generally, it is true that for any
A\in\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
the map

\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q\rightarrow\mathrm{Bin}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q

is an isomorphism. Indeed, again by passing to associated graded we reduce to
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}^{i}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q\rightarrow\Gamma_{\mathbb Z}^{i}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q
being an isomorphism for any
i
. This gives a natural embedding
\mathrm{Bin}(A)\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q
(by identifying the target with
\mathrm{Bin}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q
). Note that its image can be identified with the
\mathbb Z
-subalgebra generated by binomial coefficients
\binom{[a]}{n}=\frac{[a]([a]-1)\ldots([a]-n+1)}{n!}\in\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q
where
[a]\in\mathrm{Sym}^{1}_{\mathbb Z}(A)\simeq A
is the element corresponding to
a\in A
. Indeed, we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{i}\subset A\\
A_{i}\in\mathrm{Lat}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Bin}(A_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Bin}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{i}\subset A\\
A_{i}\in\mathrm{Lat}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A)}

and for
A\in\mathrm{Lat}
it is true by
2.18
(
4
).

Let
A\in\CAlg(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}})
be a commutative ring in
\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}}
. Then
A
embeds into
A\otimes\mathbb Q
and for any
a\in A
the binomial coefficient
\binom{a}{n}\in A\otimes\mathbb Q
is well defined. We say that
A
is
binomially closed
if
\binom{a}{n}
in fact lies in
A
for all
a\in A
and
n\geq 1
.

Proposition 2.29
.

Let
A\in\CAlg(\mathrm{Ab}^{\mathrm{tf}})
. Then
A
has a binomial ring structure if and only if it is binomially closed, in which case the binomial ring structure is unique.

Proof.

Let
A
be a binomial ring. Then we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Bin}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{A\otimes\mathbb Q}

(by identifying the bottom horizontal arrow with
\mathrm{Bin}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q\rightarrow A\otimes\mathbb Q
) with all vertical arrows being embeddings. Identifying
\mathrm{Bin}(A)\subset\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb Z}(A)\otimes\mathbb Q
with the
\mathbb Z
-subalgebra generated by
\binom{[a]}{n}
for
[a]\in\mathrm{Sym}^{1}_{\mathbb Z}(A)
we get that
\binom{a}{n}=m(\binom{[a]}{n})
lies in
A
for any
a\in A
and
n
. Thus
A
is binomially closed. We also get that the map
\mathrm{Bin}(A)\rightarrow A
